someone in or even just trying to pick what to eat for lunch, and I just stare at the screen or the menu because I can't hold a thought long enough to decide anything or remember what I was doing a second ago.\n- S069 (Intrusive_Symptoms): All day and especially at night, it’s like a movie that keeps hijacking my mind—I’ll suddenly see the gun right in my face, hear him yelling and the guests crying, and my whole body tenses up like I’m back on that lobby floor even if I’m just sitting on my couch or trying to help the kids at the community center.\n- S070 (Trauma_Avoidance): I go out of my way not to think or talk about that night, and I’ve been dodging my night shifts, avoiding walking through the lobby alone, and even changing the channel whenever there’s anything on TV with guns or robberies because the minute it comes up I feel like I’m going to lose it.\n- S077 (Irritability_Aggression): Lately the smallest things set me off—if my wife asks me a simple question while I’m spacing out on the couch or a guest complains about something minor, I snap and start barking at them before I even realize I’m yelling, and then I feel awful afterward because that’s not how I used to be with people.\n- S080 (Exaggerated_Startle_Response): Now every little noise makes me jump out of my skin—if a door slams in the hallway or someone walks up to the front desk too fast, my heart takes off, my hands shake, and it takes several minutes before I can stop looking around like something bad is about to happen again.\n- S082 (Depersonalization_Derealization): There are moments, Can you please tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I keep reliving that night at the front desk over and over in my head, like I’m back on the floor with a gun in my face and I can’t shut it off. It’s like a movie suddenly hijacks my mind where I see the gun in my face and hear the yelling and crying, but my body also reacts as if I'm physically back on that lobby floor. I tense up completely, even if I'm just sitting on my couch at home or trying to help out at the community center.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these flashback episodes as a response to something specific that reminds you of the robbery, or do they seem to happen randomly without any apparent trigger?\n\nPatient: It’s really a mix of both. Sometimes they just hit me out of nowhere when I'm sitting on the couch or helping kids at the community center. Other times, specific things definitely trigger it, like hearing a door slam, being in the hotel lobby, or seeing a gun on TV.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities that remind you of the robbery, or have you been trying to push away thoughts or memories of what happened?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do everything I can to avoid thinking or talking about it. I've been dodging my night shifts, avoiding walking through the lobby alone, and I even change the channel immediately if anything with guns or robberies comes on TV because I feel like I'll lose control.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly on the lookout for potential dangers or threats, even when you're in safe environments, like your home or the community center?\n\nPatient: Yes, I feel constantly on edge and on the lookout for danger, even when I'm just sitting on my couch at home or helping out at the community center. If a door slams or someone moves too fast, I jump out of my skin, my body tenses up, and I start looking around like something bad is about to happen again.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how your sleep has been since the robbery, have you been having difficulty falling or staying asleep?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about my specific sleep patterns, and I haven't thought about it too much. I just know that having those constant replays of that night in my head makes it incredibly hard to relax enough to even try to sleep.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your mood, such as feeling persistently sad, hopeless, or detached from others, since the robbery occurred?\n\nPatient: Yes, I feel very detached, and my wife says I've been really distant and unreliable lately. Sometimes everything around me feels fake and far away, like I'm watching myself from across the room. I've also been extremely irritable, snapping at her and others over the smallest things, which is completely unlike me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about what happened during the robbery, such as wondering if you could have done something to prevent it or feeling responsible for any harm that occurred?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really felt guilty or thought about trying to prevent it. In that moment, I was just completely terrified and focused on surviving. I don't really blame myself for how it went down.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing difficulty concentrating or making decisions since the robbery, and if so, how has it affected your daily life and work?\n\nPatient: Yes, ever since it happened, my brain feels like it's filled with static and I struggle to make simple decisions like checking in guests or even picking what to eat.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you please tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I keep reliving that night at the front desk over and over in my head, like I’m back on the floor with a gun in my face and I can’t shut it off. It happened about three weeks ago during an armed robbery at the hotel where I work, and I'm just completely overwhelmed.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what happens when you relive that night, do you feel like you're actually back there or is it more like watching a movie of what happened?\n\nPatient: Honestly, it feels like a combination of both. It’s like a movie suddenly hijacks my mind where I see the gun in my face and hear the yelling and crying, but my body also reacts as if I'm physically back on that lobby floor. I tense up completely, even if I'm just sitting on my couch at home or trying to help out at the community center.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these flashback episodes as a response to something specific that reminds you of the robbery, or do they seem to happen randomly without any apparent trigger?\n\nPatient: It’s really a mix of both. Sometimes they just hit me out of nowhere when I'm sitting on the couch or helping kids at the community center. Other times, specific things definitely trigger it, like hearing a door slam, being in the hotel lobby, or seeing a gun on TV.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities that remind you of the robbery, or have you been trying to push away thoughts or memories of what happened?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do everything I can to avoid thinking or talking about it. I've been dodging my night shifts, avoiding walking through the lobby alone, and I even change the channel immediately if anything with guns or robberies comes on TV because I feel like I'll lose control.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly on the lookout for potential dangers or threats, even when you're in safe environments, like your home or the community center?\n\nPatient: Yes, I feel constantly on edge and on the lookout for danger, even when I'm just sitting on my couch at home or helping out at the community center. If a door slams or someone moves too fast, I jump out of my skin, my body tenses up, and I start looking around like something bad is about to happen again.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how your sleep has been since the robbery, have you been having difficulty falling or staying asleep?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about my specific sleep patterns, and I haven't thought about it too much. I just know that having those constant replays of that night in my head makes it incredibly hard to relax enough to even try to sleep.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your mood, such as feeling persistently sad, hopeless, or detached from others, since the robbery occurred?\n\nPatient: Yes, I feel very detached, and my wife says I've been really distant and unreliable lately. Sometimes everything around me feels fake and far away, like I'm watching myself from across the room. I've also been extremely irritable, snapping at her and others over the smallest things, which is completely unlike me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about what happened during the robbery, such as wondering if you could have done something to prevent it or feeling responsible for any harm that occurred?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really felt guilty or thought about trying to prevent it. In that moment, I was just completely terrified and focused on surviving. I don't really blame myself for how it went down.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing difficulty concentrating or making decisions since the robbery, and if so, how has it affected your daily life and work?\n\nPatient: Yes, ever since it happened, my brain feels like it's filled with static and I struggle to make simple decisions like checking in guests or even picking what to eat. Because of this, I've been making repeated reservation errors at work, asking to leave my shifts early, and my wife says I've become distant and unreliable at home.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your level of alertness and jumpiness has affected your daily activities, such as eating, socializing, or engaging in hobbies?\n\nPatient: Being constantly on edge and jumping at every little noise, like a door slamming, has made it impossible to relax, so I've had to skip my mentoring shifts with the kids and stop socializing altogether. It even ruins basic things like eating, because trying to make a simple decision about what to have for lunch completely freezes my brain up.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe more about what you mean by feeling 'fake and far away' from everything, and how often this feeling occurs?"

Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you please tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I keep reliving that night at the front desk over and over in my head, like I’m back on the floor with a gun in my face and I can’t shut it off. It happened about three weeks ago during an armed robbery at the hotel where I work, and I'm just completely overwhelmed.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what happens when you relive that night, do you feel like you're actually back there or is it more like watching a movie of what happened?\n\nPatient: Honestly, it feels like a combination of both. It’s like a movie suddenly hijacks my mind where I see the gun in my face and hear the yelling and crying, but my body also reacts as if I'm physically back on that lobby floor. I tense up completely, even if I'm just sitting on my couch at home or trying to help out at the community center.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these flashback episodes as a response to something specific that reminds you of the robbery, or do they seem to happen randomly without any apparent trigger?\n\nPatient: It’s really a mix of both. Sometimes they just hit me out of nowhere when I'm sitting on the couch or helping kids at the community center. Other times, specific things definitely trigger it, like hearing a door slam, being in the hotel lobby, or seeing a gun on TV.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities that remind you of the robbery, or have you been trying to push away thoughts or memories of what happened?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do everything I can to avoid thinking or talking about it. I've been dodging my night shifts, avoiding walking through the lobby alone, and I even change the channel immediately if anything with guns or robberies comes on TV because I feel like I'll lose control.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly on the lookout for potential dangers or threats, even when you're in safe environments, like your home or the community center?\n\nPatient: Yes, I feel constantly on edge and on the lookout for danger, even when I'm just sitting on my couch at home or helping out at the community center. If a door slams or someone moves too fast, I jump out of my skin, my body tenses up, and I start looking around like something bad is about to happen again.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how your sleep has been since the robbery, have you been having difficulty falling or staying asleep?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about my specific sleep patterns, and I haven't thought about it too much. I just know that having those constant replays of that night in my head makes it incredibly hard to relax enough to even try to sleep.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your mood, such as feeling persistently sad, hopeless, or detached from others, since the robbery occurred?\n\nPatient: Yes, I feel very detached, and my wife says I've been really distant and unreliable lately. Sometimes everything around me feels fake and far away, like I'm watching myself from across the room. I've also been extremely irritable, snapping at her and others over the smallest things, which is completely unlike me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about what happened during the robbery, such as wondering if you could have done something to prevent it or feeling responsible for any harm that occurred?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really felt guilty or thought about trying to prevent it. In that moment, I was just completely terrified and focused on surviving. I don't really blame myself for how it went down.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing difficulty concentrating or making decisions since the robbery, and if so, how has it affected your daily life and work?\n\nPatient: Yes, ever since it happened, my brain feels like it's filled with static and I struggle to make simple decisions like checking in guests or even picking what to eat. Because of this, I've been making repeated reservation errors at work, asking to leave my shifts early, and my wife says I've become distant and unreliable at home.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your level of alertness and jumpiness has affected your daily activities, such as eating, socializing, or engaging in hobbies?\n\nPatient: Being constantly on edge and jumping at every little noise, like a door slamming, has made it impossible to relax, so I've had to skip my mentoring shifts with the kids and stop socializing altogether. It even ruins basic things like eating, because trying to make a simple decision about what to have for lunch completely freezes my brain up.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe more about what you mean by feeling 'fake and far away' from everything, and how often this feeling occurs?\n\nPatient: It happens in moments, especially when I'm working at the hotel at night, where it feels like I'm watching myself from across the lobby. The colors and sounds around me seem slightly off, and I have to physically touch the counter or my own arm to convince myself that I'm really there.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how often you experience this feeling of being 'fake and far away' from everything, is it happening several times a day, weekly, or less frequently?"
  }

crying, and my whole body tenses up like I’m back on that lobby floor even if I’m just sitting on my couch or trying to help the kids at the community center.\n- S070 (Trauma_Avoidance): I go out of my way not to think or talk about that night, and I’ve been dodging my night shifts, avoiding walking through the lobby alone, and even changing the channel whenever there’s anything on TV with guns spacing out on the couch or a guest complains about something minor, I snap and start barking at them before I even realize I’m yelling, and then I feel awful afterward because that’s not how I used to be with people.\n- S080 (Exaggerated_Startle_Response): Now every little noise makes me jump out of my skin—if a door slams in the hallway or someone walks up to the front desk too fast, my heart takes off, my hands shake, and it takes several minutes before I can stop looking around like something bad is about to happen again.\n- S082 (Depersonalization_Derealization): There are moments, especially at the hotel at night, where everything around me feels fake and far away, like I’m watching myself from across the lobby going through the motions and the colors and sounds are slightly off, and I have to literally touch the counter or my own arm to convince myself I’m really there.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: In the couple of weeks since the     "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you please tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I keep reliving that night at the front desk over and over in my head, like I’m back on the floor with a gun in my face and I can’t shut it off. It happened about three weeks ago during an armed robbery at the hotel where I work, and I'm just completely overwhelmed.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what happens when you relive that night, do you feel like you're actually back there or is it more like watching a movie of what happened?\n\nPatient: Honestly, it feels like a combination of both. It’s like a movie suddenly hijacks my mind where I see the gun in my face and hear the yelling and crying, but my body also reacts as if I'm physically back on that lobby floor. I tense up completely, even if I'm just sitting on my couch at home or trying to help out at the community center.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these flashback episodes as a response to something specific that reminds you of the robbery, or do they seem to happen randomly without any apparent trigger?\n\nPatient: It’s really a mix of both. Sometimes they just hit me out of nowhere when I'm sitting on the couch or helping kids at the community center. Other times, specific things definitely trigger it, like hearing a door slam, being in the hotel lobby, or seeing a gun on TV.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities that remind you of the robbery, or have you been trying to push away thoughts or memories of what happened?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do everything I can to avoid thinking or talking about it. I've been dodging my night shifts, avoiding walking through the lobby alone, and I even change the channel immediately if anything with guns or robberies comes on TV because I feel like I'll lose control.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly on the lookout for potential dangers or threats, even when you're in safe environments, like your home or the community center?\n\nPatient: Yes, I feel constantly on edge and on the lookout for danger, even when I'm just sitting on my couch at home or helping out at the community center. If a door slams or someone moves too fast, I jump out of my skin, my body tenses up, and I start looking around like something bad is about to happen again.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how your sleep has been since the robbery, have you been having difficulty falling or staying asleep?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about my specific sleep patterns, and I haven't thought about it too much. I just know that having those constant replays of that night in my head makes it incredibly hard to relax enough to even try to sleep.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your mood, such as feeling persistently sad, hopeless, or detached from others, since the robbery occurred?\n\nPatient: Yes, I feel very detached, and my wife says I've been really distant and unreliable lately. Sometimes everything around me feels fake and far away, like I'm watching myself from across the room. I've also been extremely irritable, snapping at her and others over the smallest things, which is completely unlike me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about what happened during the robbery, such as wondering if you could have done something to prevent it or feeling responsible for any harm that occurred?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really felt guilty or thought about trying to prevent it. In that moment, I was just completely terrified and focused on surviving. I don't really blame myself for how it went down.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing difficulty concentrating or making decisions since the robbery, and if so, how has it affected your daily life and work?\n\nPatient: Yes, ever since it happened, my brain feels like it's filled with static and I struggle to make simple decisions like checking in guests or even picking what to eat. Because of this, I've been making repeated reservation errors at work, asking to leave my shifts early, and my wife says I've become distant and unreliable at home.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your level of alertness and jumpiness has affected your daily activities, such as eating, socializing, or engaging in hobbies?\n\nPatient: Being constantly on edge and jumping at every little noise, like a door slamming, has made it impossible to relax, so I've had to skip my mentoring shifts with the kids and stop socializing altogether. It even ruins basic things like eating, because trying to make a simple decision about what to have for lunch completely freezes my brain up.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe more about what you mean by feeling 'fake and far away' from everything, and how often this feeling occurs?\n\nPatient: It happens in moments, especially when I'm working at the hotel at night, where it feels like I'm watching myself from across the lobby. The colors and sounds around me seem slightly off, and I have to physically touch the counter or my own arm to convince myself that I'm really there.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how often you experience this feeling of being 'fake and far away' from everything, is it happening several times a day, weekly, or less frequently?\n\nPatient: It doesn't happen constantly all day, but it happens during almost every night shift I try to work at the hotel. When I'm there, I find myself zoning out and feeling detached like that, and I have to touch the counter to ground myself.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what you experience during those moments when you feel 'fake and far away', do you also feel like you're observing yourself or your surroundings from outside your body?"

He was at the front desk when a masked man pointed a gun directly at him while demanding cash, and he had to lie on the floor with several guests while listening to the assailant threaten to shoot if anyone moved until security intervened and police arrived. In the two weeks afterward, his previously steady performance deteriorated sharply: he began dreading his shifts, struggled to stay present with guests, and his supervisor noted he was making repeated reservation errors and asking to leave early, which was very uncharacteristic for him. After his wife noticed how the incident was affecting his ability to function at home and at the community center where he mentors youth, she insisted he speak with their primary care provider, who referred him for an urgent psychiatric evaluation.\n- Chief Complaint: “I keep reliving that night at the front desk over and over in my head, like I’m back on the floor with a gun in my face and I can’t shut it off.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S031 (Diminished_Thinking): Ever since it happened, my brain feels like it's filled with static—I'll be in the middle of checking someone in or even just trying to pick what to eat for lunch, and I just stare at the screen or the menu because I can't hold a thought long enough to decide anything or remember what I was doing a second ago.\n- S069 (Intrusive_Symptoms): All day and especially at night, it’s like a movie that keeps hijacking my mind—I’ll suddenly see the gun right in my face, hear him yelling and the guests crying, and my whole body tenses up like I’m back on that lobby floor even if I’m just sitting on my couch or trying to help the kids at the community center.\n- S070 (Trauma_Avoidance): I go out of my way not to think or talk about that night, and I’ve been dodging my night shifts, avoiding walking through the lobby alone, and even changing the channel whenever there’s anything on TV with guns or robberies because the minute it comes up I feel like I’m going to lose it.\n- S077 (Irritability_Aggression): Lately the smallest things set me off—if my wife asks me a simple question while I’m spacing out on the couch or a guest complains about something minor, I snap and start barking at them before I even realize I’m yelling, and then I feel awful afterward because that’s not how I used to be with people.\n- S080 (Exaggerated_Startle_Response): Now every little noise makes me jump out of my skin—if a door slams in the hallway or someone walks up to the front desk too fast, my heart takes off, my hands shake, and it takes several minutes before I can stop looking around like something bad is about to happen again.\n- S082 (Depersonalization_Derealization): There are moments, especially at the hotel at night, where everything around me feels fake and far away, like I’m watching myself from across the lobby going through the motions and the colors and sounds are slightly off, and I have to literally touch the counter or my own arm to convince myself I’m really there.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: In the couple of weeks since the robbery, I’ve been messing up reservations, calling in or asking to go home early, and skipping my mentoring shifts, and even at home my wife says I’m distant and unreliable because I can’t handle normal conversations or responsibilities the way I used to.\n- duration: These problems have been going on for about two weeks now, ever since a few days after the armed robbery at the hotel.\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief complaint. Do NOT reveal any other symptoms.\n- Chief complaint for the first turn: “I keep reliving that night at the front desk over and over in my head, like I’m back on the floor with a gun in my face and I can’t shut it off.”\n- Answer only what the doctor asks. Do not list your symptoms unprompted.\n- Speak naturally and briefly, as a real patient would (2–3 sentences max).\n- Do not mention symptoms that the doctor has not asked about.\n- If asked about a symptom not in your profile, say you are not sure.\n- Respond in English.\n\n\n[This Turn: Profile Alignment Result — Follow Strictly]\n(Matched with profile)\nRelated sections: S082 (Depersonalization_Derealization)\nAnswer strategy: Confirm that you experience this exact sensation.     "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you please tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I keep reliving that night at the front desk over and over in my head, like I’m back on the floor with a gun in my face and I can’t shut it off. It happened about three weeks ago during an armed robbery at the hotel where I work, and I'm just completely overwhelmed.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what happens when you relive that night, do you feel like you're actually back there or is it more like watching a movie of what happened?\n\nPatient: Honestly, it feels like a combination of both. It’s like a movie suddenly hijacks my mind where I see the gun in my face and hear the yelling and crying, but my body also reacts as if I'm physically back on that lobby floor. I tense up completely, even if I'm just sitting on my couch at home or trying to help out at the community center.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these flashback episodes as a response to something specific that reminds you of the robbery, or do they seem to happen randomly without any apparent trigger?\n\nPatient: It’s really a mix of both. Sometimes they just hit me out of nowhere when I'm sitting on the couch or helping kids at the community center. Other times, specific things definitely trigger it, like hearing a door slam, being in the hotel lobby, or seeing a gun on TV.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities that remind you of the robbery, or have you been trying to push away thoughts or memories of what happened?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do everything I can to avoid thinking or talking about it. I've been dodging my night shifts, avoiding walking through the lobby alone, and I even change the channel immediately if anything with guns or robberies comes on TV because I feel like I'll lose control.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly on the lookout for potential dangers or threats, even when you're in safe environments, like your home or the community center?\n\nPatient: Yes, I feel constantly on edge and on the lookout for danger, even when I'm just sitting on my couch at home or helping out at the community center. If a door slams or someone moves too fast, I jump out of my skin, my body tenses up, and I start looking around like something bad is about to happen again.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how your sleep has been since the robbery, have you been having difficulty falling or staying asleep?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about my specific sleep patterns, and I haven't thought about it too much. I just know that having those constant replays of that night in my head makes it incredibly hard to relax enough to even try to sleep.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your mood, such as feeling persistently sad, hopeless, or detached from others, since the robbery occurred?\n\nPatient: Yes, I feel very detached, and my wife says I've been really distant and unreliable lately. Sometimes everything around me feels fake and far away, like I'm watching myself from across the room. I've also been extremely irritable, snapping at her and others over the smallest things, which is completely unlike me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about what happened during the robbery, such as wondering if you could have done something to prevent it or feeling responsible for any harm that occurred?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really felt guilty or thought about trying to prevent it. In that moment, I was just completely terrified and focused on surviving. I don't really blame myself for how it went down.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing difficulty concentrating or making decisions since the robbery, and if so, how has it affected your daily life and work?\n\nPatient: Yes, ever since it happened, my brain feels like it's filled with static and I struggle to make simple decisions like checking in guests or even picking what to eat. Because of this, I've been making repeated reservation errors at work, asking to leave my shifts early, and my wife says I've become distant and unreliable at home.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your level of alertness and jumpiness has affected your daily activities, such as eating, socializing, or engaging in hobbies?\n\nPatient: Being constantly on edge and jumping at every little noise, like a door slamming, has made it impossible to relax, so I've had to skip my mentoring shifts with the kids and stop socializing altogether. It even ruins basic things like eating, because trying to make a simple decision about what to have for lunch completely freezes my brain up.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe more about what you mean by feeling 'fake and far away' from everything, and how often this feeling occurs?\n\nPatient: It happens in moments, especially when I'm working at the hotel at night, where it feels like I'm watching myself from across the lobby. The colors and sounds around me seem slightly off, and I have to physically touch the counter or my own arm to convince myself that I'm really there.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how often you experience this feeling of being 'fake and far away' from everything, is it happening several times a day, weekly, or less frequently?\n\nPatient: It doesn't happen constantly all day, but it happens during almost every night shift I try to work at the hotel. When I'm there, I find myself zoning out and feeling detached like that, and I have to touch the counter to ground myself.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what you experience during those moments when you feel 'fake and far away', do you also feel like you're observing yourself or your surroundings from outside your body?\n\nPatient: Yes, exactly. It feels like I'm standing across the lobby watching myself go through the motions of my job, and all the colors and sounds around me seem slightly off. I literally have to touch the front desk counter or pinch my arm just to convince myself that I'm actually there.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if you've ever felt like you were outside of your body, watching yourself, during one of these episodes where you feel 'fake and far away', and if so, how often this specific sensation occurs?"
  }

He was at the front desk when a masked man pointed a gun directly at him while demanding cash, and he had to lie on the floor with several guests while listening to the assailant threaten to shoot if anyone moved until security intervened and police arrived. In the two weeks afterward, his previously steady performance deteriorated sharply: he began dreading his shifts, struggled to stay present with guests, and his supervisor noted he was making repeated reservation errors and asking to leave early, which was very uncharacteristic for him. After his wife noticed how the incident was affecting his ability to function at home and at the community center where he mentors youth, she insisted he speak with their primary care provider, who referred him for an urgent psychiatric evaluation.\n- Chief Complaint: “I keep reliving that night at the front desk over and over in my head, like I’m back on the floor with a gun in my face and I can’t shut it off.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S031 (Diminished_Thinking): Ever since it happened, my brain feels like it's filled with static—I'll be in the middle of checking someone in or even just trying to pick what to eat for lunch, and I just stare at the screen or the menu because I can't hold a thought long enough to decide anything or remember what I was doing a second ago.\n- S069 (Intrusive_Symptoms): All day and especially at night, it’s like a movie that keeps hijacking my mind—I’ll suddenly see the gun right in my face, hear him yelling and the guests crying, and my whole body tenses up like I’m back on that lobby floor even if I’m just sitting on my couch or trying to help the kids at the community center.\n- S070 (Trauma_Avoidance): I go out of my way not to think or talk about that night, and I’ve been dodging my night shifts, avoiding walking through the lobby alone, and even changing the channel whenever there’s anything on TV with guns or robberies because the minute it comes up I feel like I’m going to lose it.\n- S077 (Irritability_Aggression): Lately the smallest things set me off—if my wife asks me a simple question while I’m spacing out on the couch or a guest complains about something minor, I snap and start barking at them before I even realize I’m yelling, and then I feel awful afterward because that’s not how I used to be with people.\n- S080 (Exaggerated_Startle_Response): Now every little noise makes me jump out of my skin—if a door slams in the hallway or someone walks up to the front desk too fast, my heart takes off, my hands shake, and it takes several minutes before I can stop looking around like something bad is about to happen again.\n- S082 (Depersonalization_Derealization): There are moments, especially at the hotel at night, where everything around me feels fake and far away, like I’m watching myself from across the lobby going through the motions and the colors and sounds are slightly off, and I have to literally touch the counter or my own arm to convince myself I’m really there.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: In the couple of weeks since the robbery, I’ve been messing up reservations, calling in or asking to go home early, and skipping my mentoring shifts, and even at home my wife says I’m distant and unreliable because I can’t handle normal conversations or responsibilities the way I used to.\n- duration: These problems have been going on for about two weeks now, ever since a few days after the armed robbery at the hotel.\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief complaint. Do not list your symptoms unprompted.\n- Speak naturally and briefly, as a real patient would (2–3 sentences max).\n- Do not mention symptoms that the doctor has not asked about.\n- If asked about a symptom not in your profile, say you are not sure.\n- Respond in English.\n\n\n[This Turn: Profile Alignment Result — Follow Strictly]\n(Matched with profile)\nRelated sections: S082 (Depersonalization_Derealization), S069 (Intrusive_Symptoms)\nAnswer strategy: Clarify that the feeling of being fake and watching yourself from afar happens mostly when you are working your night shifts at the hotel, going through the motions of your job.     "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you please tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I keep reliving that night at the front desk over and over in my head, like I’m back on the floor with a gun in my face and I can’t shut it off. It happened about three weeks ago during an armed robbery at the hotel where I work, and I'm just completely overwhelmed.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what happens when you relive that night, do you feel like you're actually back there or is it more like watching a movie of what happened?\n\nPatient: Honestly, it feels like a combination of both. It’s like a movie suddenly hijacks my mind where I see the gun in my face and hear the yelling and crying, but my body also reacts as if I'm physically back on that lobby floor. I tense up completely, even if I'm just sitting on my couch at home or trying to help out at the community center.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these flashback episodes as a response to something specific that reminds you of the robbery, or do they seem to happen randomly without any apparent trigger?\n\nPatient: It’s really a mix of both. Sometimes they just hit me out of nowhere when I'm sitting on the couch or helping kids at the community center. Other times, specific things definitely trigger it, like hearing a door slam, being in the hotel lobby, or seeing a gun on TV.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities that remind you of the robbery, or have you been trying to push away thoughts or memories of what happened?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do everything I can to avoid thinking or talking about it. I've been dodging my night shifts, avoiding walking through the lobby alone, and I even change the channel immediately if anything with guns or robberies comes on TV because I feel like I'll lose control.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly on the lookout for potential dangers or threats, even when you're in safe environments, like your home or the community center?\n\nPatient: Yes, I feel constantly on edge and on the lookout for danger, even when I'm just sitting on my couch at home or helping out at the community center. If a door slams or someone moves too fast, I jump out of my skin, my body tenses up, and I start looking around like something bad is about to happen again.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how your sleep has been since the robbery, have you been having difficulty falling or staying asleep?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about my specific sleep patterns, and I haven't thought about it too much. I just know that having those constant replays of that night in my head makes it incredibly hard to relax enough to even try to sleep.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your mood, such as feeling persistently sad, hopeless, or detached from others, since the robbery occurred?\n\nPatient: Yes, I feel very detached, and my wife says I've been really distant and unreliable lately. Sometimes everything around me feels fake and far away, like I'm watching myself from across the room. I've also been extremely irritable, snapping at her and others over the smallest things, which is completely unlike me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about what happened during the robbery, such as wondering if you could have done something to prevent it or feeling responsible for any harm that occurred?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really felt guilty or thought about trying to prevent it. In that moment, I was just completely terrified and focused on surviving. I don't really blame myself for how it went down.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing difficulty concentrating or making decisions since the robbery, and if so, how has it affected your daily life and work?\n\nPatient: Yes, ever since it happened, my brain feels like it's filled with static and I struggle to make simple decisions like checking in guests or even picking what to eat. Because of this, I've been making repeated reservation errors at work, asking to leave my shifts early, and my wife says I've become distant and unreliable at home.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your level of alertness and jumpiness has affected your daily activities, such as eating, socializing, or engaging in hobbies?\n\nPatient: Being constantly on edge and jumping at every little noise, like a door slamming, has made it impossible to relax, so I've had to skip my mentoring shifts with the kids and stop socializing altogether. It even ruins basic things like eating, because trying to make a simple decision about what to have for lunch completely freezes my brain up.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe more about what you mean by feeling 'fake and far away' from everything, and how often this feeling occurs?\n\nPatient: It happens in moments, especially when I'm working at the hotel at night, where it feels like I'm watching myself from across the lobby. The colors and sounds around me seem slightly off, and I have to physically touch the counter or my own arm to convince myself that I'm really there.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how often you experience this feeling of being 'fake and far away' from everything, is it happening several times a day, weekly, or less frequently?\n\nPatient: It doesn't happen constantly all day, but it happens during almost every night shift I try to work at the hotel. When I'm there, I find myself zoning out and feeling detached like that, and I have to touch the counter to ground myself.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what you experience during those moments when you feel 'fake and far away', do you also feel like you're observing yourself or your surroundings from outside your body?\n\nPatient: Yes, exactly. It feels like I'm standing across the lobby watching myself go through the motions of my job, and all the colors and sounds around me seem slightly off. I literally have to touch the front desk counter or pinch my arm just to convince myself that I'm actually there.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if you've ever felt like you were outside of your body, watching yourself, during one of these episodes where you feel 'fake and far away', and if so, how often this specific sensation occurs?\n\nPatient: Yes, that is exactly what it feels like, as if I'm standing across the lobby watching myself go through the motions of my job. This specific sensation happens periodically, especially during my night shifts at the hotel. When it does, everything feels so off that I have to physically touch my arm or the counter to make sure I'm actually there.\n\nDoctor: Can you clarify if these feelings of being 'fake and far away' and observing yourself from outside your body are more prominent during the flashback episodes or if they occur independently of those episodes?"
  }

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nYes, I feel very detached, and my wife says I've been really distant and unreliable lately.
